# MYSTICS
### OF THE
# RENAISSANCE

->[Three ornate golden crosses with decorative scrollwork and floral motifs at the ends of the arms]<-

## RUDOLF STEINER

<term>Mysticism</term> <note>A spiritual practice focused on achieving a direct, personal experience or union with the divine or absolute truth, often through internal contemplation.</note>

<term>Renaissance</term> <note>Meaning "rebirth," this was a period of intense cultural and intellectual change in Europe (roughly 14th–17th centuries) that bridge the gap between the Middle Ages and the modern era.</note>

<note>Rudolf Steiner (1861–1925) was an Austrian philosopher and social reformer. This book examines how thinkers of the Renaissance era laid the groundwork for modern spiritual and scientific understanding.</note>

<summary>The front cover of Rudolf Steiner's exploration into Renaissance spiritual thinkers, presented in a formal, gold-embossed binding.</summary>
<keywords>Rudolf Steiner, Mystics, Renaissance, mysticism, philosophy, spiritualism, Anthroposophy</keywords>

| <note>Eckhart von Hochheim (c. 1260–1328), known as "Meister" (Master) in recognition of his academic rank, was a Dominican theologian. He is a foundational figure in Western mysticism, famous for teaching that the "spark of the soul" is essentially one with the Divine.</note> | |
| FRIENDSHIP WITH GOD [TAULER, SUSO AND RUYSBROECK] . . . . . | 81 |
| <note>original: "Gottesfreundschaft." This refers to the "Friends of God" (*Gottesfreunde*), a 14th-century lay mystical movement in the Rhine valley. Johannes Tauler and Henry Suso were followers of Eckhart, while the Flemish Jan van Ruysbroeck influenced the movement toward practical devotion and internal prayer.</note> | |
| CARDINAL NICHOLAS OF CUSA . . . | 133 |
| <note>Nicholas of Cusa (Nicolaus Cusanus, 1401–1464) was a high-ranking prince-bishop and a polymath. He is celebrated for his "learned ignorance" theory—the idea that the infinite Divine cannot be grasped by logic alone—and his concept of the "coincidence of opposites."</note> | |
| AGRIPPA VON NETTESHEIM AND THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS . . . | 182 |
| <note>These figures represent the "Natural Philosophy" of the Renaissance. Agrippa explored the hidden powers of the mind, while Paracelsus, a revolutionary physician, viewed the human body as a "microcosm" that mirrors the entire universe (the "macrocosm").</note> | |
| VALENTINE WEIGEL AND JACOB BOEHME | 223 |
| <note>Jacob Boehme (1575–1624) was a simple shoemaker whose complex, visionary writings about the struggle between light and darkness in the world had a profound impact on later Western philosophy and Romanticism.</note> | |
| GIORDANO BRUNO AND ANGELUS SILESIUS | 246 |
| <note>Giordano Bruno was a radical philosopher executed for his belief in an infinite universe; Angelus Silesius (the "Silesian Angel") was a 17th-century priest who wrote "The Cherubic Wanderer," a collection of brief, powerful mystical poems.</note> | |
| AFTERWORD . . . . . . | 269 |

<meta>Following the Table of Contents, this page begins the Foreword where Steiner explains the origin of these lectures and their connection to his earlier philosophical work.</meta>

<page-num>v</page-num>
<unclear>Faint ghosting or ink bleed-through from the reverse side of the page is visible at the top.</unclear>

# FOREWORD

THE matter which I am laying before the public in this book formed the content of lectures which I delivered during last winter at the Theosophical Library in Berlin. I had been requested by Countess and Count Brockdorff <note>Count Cay von Brockdorff and his wife Sophie were prominent leaders in the Berlin branch of the Theosophical Society and hosted many of Steiner's foundational lectures.</note> to speak upon Mysticism <term>Mysticism: A spiritual path focused on the internal, direct experience of the divine or ultimate reality, often transcending logical or sensory understanding.</term> before an audience for whom the matters thus dealt with constitute a vital question of the utmost importance. Ten years earlier I could not have ventured to fulfil such a request. Not that the realm of ideas, to which I now give expression, did not even then live actively within me. For these ideas are already fully contained in my *Philosophy of Freedom* <note>original: "Philosophie der Freiheit"</note> (Berlin, 1894. Emil Felber). But to give ex-

<summary>In this opening of the Foreword, Rudolf Steiner explains that the book's content originated as lectures delivered in Berlin at the request of the Brockdorffs. He notes that while these mystical ideas were present in his earlier 1894 philosophical writings, he only recently felt ready to present them in this specific context.</summary>
<keywords>Foreword, Berlin, Theosophical Library, Countess and Count Brockdorff, Mysticism, Philosophy of Freedom, Rudolf Steiner</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the Foreword from page v, where Steiner discusses the personal development required to speak publicly about his spiritual insights and addresses potential perceived contradictions in his work.</meta>

<page-num>vi</page-num>

## FOREWORD

pression to this world of ideas <note>Steiner often uses "world of ideas" (Ideenwelt) to describe the realm of thought as a living, spiritual reality rather than just abstract concepts.</note> in such wise as I do today, and to make it the basis of an exposition as is done on the following pages—to do this requires something quite other than merely to be immovably convinced of the intellectual truth of these ideas. It demands an intimate acquaintance with this realm of ideas, such as only many years of life can give. Only now, after having enjoyed that intimacy, do I venture to speak in such wise as will be found in this book.

Any one who does not approach my world of ideas without preconceptions is sure to discover therein contradiction after contradiction. I have quite recently (Berlin, 1900. S. Cronbach) dedicated a book upon the **world conceptions** <term>World conceptions: A translation of the German "Weltanschauungen," referring to comprehensive ways of perceiving and interpreting the world and human existence.</term> of the nineteenth century to that great naturalist, Ernst Haeckel <note>Ernst Haeckel (1834–1919) was a prominent German biologist and philosopher who championed Darwinism. Steiner admired Haeckel's scientific contributions but sought to reconcile his materialist evolution with spiritual development.</note>, and closed it

<summary>Steiner emphasizes that speaking on these spiritual topics requires a "lived intimacy" with the ideas beyond mere intellectual conviction. He also acknowledges that critics may find contradictions in his work, particularly regarding his public support for the naturalist Ernst Haeckel.</summary>

<keywords>world of ideas, intellectual truth, world conceptions, nineteenth century, Ernst Haeckel, philosophy, spiritual experience</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Foreword from page vi. Steiner continues to address the perceived contradictions in his intellectual journey, specifically his ability to appreciate both the materialist scientific worldview and the spiritual insights of classical mystics.</meta>

<page-num>vii</page-num>

# FOREWORD

with a defense of his thought-world. In the following expositions, I speak about the <term>Mystics</term> <note>The term "Mystics" refers to individuals who seek a direct, personal experience of the divine or spiritual reality through inward contemplation.</note>, from <term>Master Eckhart</term> <note>Johannes Eckhart (c. 1260–1328) was a German theologian and philosopher known for his profound influence on Western spirituality and his teachings on the "spark of the soul."</note> to <term>Angelus Silesius</term> <note>Angelus Silesius (1624–1677) was a German Catholic priest and poet whose mystical aphorisms explored the intimate relationship between the human soul and God.</note>, with a full measure of devotion and acquiescence. Other "contradictions," which one critic or another may further count up against me, I shall not mention at all. It does not surprise me to be condemned from one side as a <term>Mystic</term> and from the other as a <term>Materialist</term> <note>A "Materialist" is one who believes that only physical matter exists and that spiritual or mental phenomena are merely the results of material interactions.</note>. When I find that the <term>Jesuit Father Müller</term> <note>Likely a reference to a contemporary scientist-priest; Steiner uses this example to show that truth can be found in any camp regardless of their overall dogma.</note> has solved a difficult chemical problem, and I therefore in this particular matter agree with him unreservedly, one can hardly condemn me as an adherent of <term>Jesuitism</term> <note>"Jesuitism" here refers to the specific religious and political doctrines of the Society of Jesus; Steiner argues that accepting a scientific truth from a Jesuit does not mean accepting their entire religious system.</note> without being reckoned a fool by those who have insight.

Whoever goes his own road, as I do, must needs allow many a misunderstanding about himself to pass. That,

<summary>Steiner defends his intellectual independence, explaining that he can admire both scientific materialists and spiritual mystics without contradiction. He argues that recognizing a specific truth within a specific field does not mean one adopts the entire belief system of the person who discovered it.</summary>

<keywords>Master Eckhart, Angelus Silesius, Mystics, Materialism, Jesuitism, intellectual independence, spiritual science</keywords>

without humorous feelings, upon many 
a “critical” judgment that I have suf-

At the outset, matters went fairly well. 
I wrote about <term>Goethe</term> <note>Johann Wolfgang von Goethe (1749–1832) was a monumental figure in German literature; Steiner began his career by editing and providing commentary on Goethe's scientific writings, emphasizing a holistic approach to nature.</note> and his philosophy.

of such a nature that they could file it

tation, be described as the best that has 
been written upon this question.”

When, later, I published an inde-
pendent work, I had already grown a 
good bit more stupid. For now a well

<summary>Steiner reflects with humor on his literary career, noting that critics initially praised his work on Goethe because it fit into their established categories, but became dismissive as he began to publish his own independent philosophical thoughts.</summary>

# FOREWORD

A certain critic offered the advice: 
> "Before he goes on reforming further and gives his *Philosophy of Freedom* to the world, he should be pressingly advised first to work himself through to an understanding of these two philosophers [Hume and Kant]." 

The critic unfortunately knows only so much as he is himself able to read in <term>Kant</term> <note>Immanuel Kant (1724–1804), a German philosopher who argued that human understanding is limited by the way our minds process sensory information.</note> and <term>Hume</term>; <note>David Hume (1711–1776), a Scottish philosopher known for his skepticism, particularly regarding the nature of cause and effect.</note> practically, therefore, he simply advises me to learn to see no more in these thinkers than he himself sees. When I have attained that, he will be satisfied with me. Then when my *Philosophy and Freedom* <note>original: "Philosophy and Freedom" — This likely refers to the author's primary work, "The Philosophy of Freedom" (Die Philosophie der Freiheit), though the title is slightly varied here.</note> appeared, I was found to be as much in need of correction as the most ignorant beginner. This I received from a gentleman who probably nothing else impelled to the writing of books except that he had not understood innumerable foreign ones. He gravely informs me that I should have

<summary>In this foreword, Rudolf Steiner defends his work against critics who claimed he lacked a proper understanding of Kant and Hume, arguing that his detractors only want him to mirror their own limited interpretations.</summary>
<keywords>Rudolf Steiner, Immanuel Kant, David Hume, Philosophy of Freedom, philosophical criticism, German Idealism, epistemology</keywords>

# FOREWORD

noticed my mistakes if I had “made
more thorough studies in <term>psychology</term>, <note>At the time of writing, psychology was closely tied to philosophy, focusing on how the human mind experiences and processes reality.</note>
<term>logic</term>, and the <term>theory of knowledge</term>”; <note>Also known as epistemology, this is the branch of philosophy that investigates the nature, origin, and limits of human knowledge.</note>
and he enumerates forthwith the books
I ought to read to become as wise as
himself: “<term>Mill</term>, <note>John Stuart Mill (1806–1873), an English philosopher who argued that all knowledge comes from sensory experience.</note> <term>Sigwart</term>, <note>Christoph von Sigwart (1830–1904), a German philosopher known for his influential work on the rules of logical thinking.</note> <term>Wundt</term>, <note>Wilhelm Wundt (1832–1920), a pioneer who helped establish psychology as a science separate from philosophy.</note> <term>Riehl</term>, <note>Alois Riehl (1844–1924), a philosopher who sought to ground philosophy in scientific principles.</note>
<term>Paulsen</term>, <note>Friedrich Paulsen (1846–1908), a German thinker known for his work on ethics and the history of education.</note> <term>B. Erdmann</term>.” <note>Benno Erdmann (1851–1921), a philosopher and logician specialized in the works of Kant.</note> What amused
me especially was this advice from a
man who was so “impressed” with the
way he “understood” <term>Kant</term> <note>Immanuel Kant (1724–1804), the central figure of modern philosophy who argued that we cannot know "things-in-themselves," only how they appear to us.</note> that he
could not even imagine how any man
could have read Kant and yet judge
otherwise than himself. He therefore
indicates to me the exact chapters in
question in Kant’s writings from which
I may be able to obtain an understanding
of Kant as deep and as thorough as
his own.

I have cited here a couple of typical
criticisms of my world of ideas. Though
in themselves unimportant, yet they

<summary>Steiner recounts how a critic patronizingly listed several contemporary philosophers he should study, illustrating the dogmatic arrogance of those who believed their specific interpretation of Kant was the only valid one.</summary>

->xi<-

# FOREWORD

seem to me to point, as symptoms, to facts which present themselves to-day as serious obstacles in the path of any one aiming at literary activity in regard to the higher problems of knowledge. Thus I must go on my way, indifferent, whether one man gives me the good advice to read <term>Kant</term>, <note>Immanuel Kant (1724–1804) was a landmark German philosopher who argued that the human mind shapes our experience of reality, though he believed "things-in-themselves" remained unknowable.</note> or another hunts me as a heretic because I agree with <term>Haeckel</term>. <note>Ernst Haeckel (1834–1919) was an influential German biologist and philosopher who championed Darwin’s theories and sought a scientific explanation for all of life's mysteries.</note> And so I have also written upon <term>Mysticism</term>, <note>In this context, mysticism refers to the pursuit of direct, inner spiritual experience rather than just intellectual or sensory knowledge.</note> wholly indifferent as to how a faithful and believing <term>materialist</term> <note>A philosopher who believes that physical matter is the only reality and that spiritual or mental phenomena are just products of material interactions.</note> may judge of me. I would only like—so that printers' ink may not be wasted wholly without need—to inform any one who may, perchance advise me to read Haeckel’s *Riddle of the Universe*, <note>First published in 1899 as *Die Welträthsel*, this work was an international bestseller that promoted a scientific, evolutionary worldview over traditional religious dogmas.</note> that during the last few months I have delivered about thirty lectures upon the said work.

I hope to have shown in this book that one may be a faithful adherent of

<summary>Steiner asserts his intellectual independence, dismissing critics who label him either a heretic for supporting scientific thinkers like Haeckel or a mystic for his spiritual interests.</summary>
<keywords>Rudolf Steiner, Immanuel Kant, Ernst Haeckel, Mysticism, Materialism, Riddle of the Universe, intellectual independence, philosophy of science</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues from page xi, where Steiner expressed his independence from critics and mentioned his lectures on Ernst Haeckel's "Riddle of the Universe." He now concludes the foreword by reconciling the scientific worldview with spiritual practice.</meta>

->xii<-
->FOREWORD<-

## FOREWORD

the scientific conception of the world and yet be able to seek out those paths to the <term>Soul</term> <note>In Steiner's philosophy, the soul is the inner realm of feeling and character that bridges our physical senses and our spiritual consciousness.</note> along which <term>Mysticism</term>, <note>Steiner defines true mysticism not as vague dreaming, but as a disciplined method of gaining direct knowledge of spiritual realities.</note> rightly understood, leads. I even go further and say: Only he who knows the <term>Spirit</term>, <note>The Spirit refers to the objective, eternal reality that exists behind the physical world.</note> in the sense of true Mysticism, can attain a full understanding of the facts of <term>Nature</term>. <note>Nature here refers to the physical world governed by the laws of physics and biology.</note> But one must not confuse true Mysticism with the "pseudo-mysticism" of ill-ordered minds. How Mysticism can err, I have shown in my *Philosophy of Freedom* <note>First published in 1894, this is Steiner's foundational work on the nature of human thinking and spiritual liberty.</note> (page 131 *et seq.* <note>Latin: "and the following." Referring to page 131 and the pages following it.</note>).

->RUDOLF STEINER.<-

BERLIN, *September*, 1901.

## INTRODUCTION

THERE are certain magical <term>formulas</term> <note>original: "formulæ." Steiner uses this term to describe potent, concentrated ideas or maxims that act as "keys" to unlocking spiritual understanding across different eras.</note> which operate throughout the centuries of humanity’s mental history in ever new ways. In Greece, one such formula was regarded as an oracle of the god Apollo. It runs: “Know Thyself.” <note>original Greek: "Gnothi Seauton." This famous inscription from the Temple of Delphi serves as the starting point for Steiner's investigation into how the soul relates to the wider world.</note> Such sentences seem to conceal within them an unending life. One comes upon them when following the most diverse roads in mental life. The further one advances, the more one penetrates into the knowledge of things, the deeper appears the significance of these <term>formulas</term>. In many a moment of our brooding and thinking, they flash

Whoever calls into question this content, shows only that he is lacking in this inner experience. It is impossible to dispute with him; as little could one discuss colour with a blind man.

# INTRODUCTION

Asmus<note>Paul Asmus (1842–1876) was a German philosopher known for his work on the relationship between the human ego and the external world, often through a Hegelian lens.</note>, the keen-sighted philosopher, who died young, expressed himself as follows about this position (*cp.* his book <note>original: "Das Ich und das Ding an Sich"</note> *The I and the Thing-in-Itself*, p. 14 *et seq.*):—

“Let us make it clear by an example: imagine a piece of sugar; it is square, sweet, impenetrable, etc., etc., these are one and all qualities which we understand; one thing, however, hovers before us as something totally different, that we do not understand, that is so different from ourselves that we cannot penetrate into it without losing ourselves; from the mere surface of which thought starts back afraid. This one thing is the unknown bearer of all these qualities; the <term>thing-in-itself</term><note>A translation of the German "Ding an sich," a central concept in philosophy (notably Immanuel Kant's) referring to the reality of an object as it exists independently of our senses and perceptions.</note>, which constitutes the inmost self of the object. Thus Hegel<note>Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel (1770–1831), a titan of German Idealism, who argued that reality is ultimately rational and that the "thing-in-itself" is not an unknowable mystery but something that can be understood through the development of Spirit and logic.</note> rightly says that the entire content of our perception is related as mere <term>accident</term><note>In philosophical logic, an "accident" is a property that a thing possesses but which is not essential to its nature; for example, being "sweet" is an attribute of the sugar, but the "subject" or essence lies deeper.</note> to this obscure subject, while we,

<summary>The author quotes philosopher Paul Asmus to illustrate the gap between the properties we perceive (like the sweetness of sugar) and the "thing-in-itself," which remains an "obscure subject" to everyday thought.</summary>

<page-num>46</page-num>
# MYSTICS OF THE RENAISSANCE

For all action that is not accomplished in the light of <term>self-knowledge</term><note>In this context, self-knowledge refers to the conscious awareness of why one is thinking or acting in a specific way.</note>, the motive—the reason for action—must needs be felt as a constraint. <note>This "constraint" is the feeling that we are being pushed by forces outside of our conscious control, such as instincts or societal pressure.</note> But the matter is otherwise when the reason or motive is taken up into self-knowledge. Then this reason becomes a part of the self. The <term>willing</term><note>The mental process of making a choice or a decision; the act of exercising the will.</note> is no longer determined; it determines itself. The <term>law-abidingness</term><note>The internal logic or "rules" that govern how a person chooses to act.</note>, the motives of willing, now no longer rule over the one who wills, but are one and the same with this willing. To illuminate the laws of one’s action with the light of self-observation means to overcome all constraint of motive. By so doing, will transfers itself into the realm of freedom.

It is not all human action which bears the marks of freedom. Only such action is free action which in its every part is lighted up with the glow of self-observation—

| **Core Concept** | **Definition in Context** |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Self-knowledge** | The transformation of an unconscious impulse into a conscious thought. |
| **Self-observation** | The act of watching one's own mental processes as they happen. |
| **Freedom** | Not the absence of motives, but the state where the motive and the self are one. |
| **Motive** | The "fuel" or reason for an action; becomes a "free cause" once understood. |

<summary>This page argues that human freedom is achieved when we consciously understand the motives behind our actions, rather than being blindly driven by them. When we observe ourselves, our reasons for acting become part of our own identity, transforming necessity into freedom.</summary>
<keywords>self-knowledge, self-observation, freedom of the will, motives, self-determination, Rudolf Steiner, Renaissance philosophy</keywords>

## MEISTER ECKHART

THE world of Meister Eckhart's conceptions <note>The set of ideas or mental frameworks through which Eckhart understood reality.</note> is aglow through and through with the feeling that things become reborn as higher entities in the spirit of man. Like the greatest Christian theologian of the Middle Ages, St. Thomas Aquinas, who lived from 1225 till 1274, Meister Eckhart belonged to the Dominican Order <note>Known as the Order of Preachers, this Catholic religious order emphasized intellectual study and preaching.</note>. Eckhart was an unqualified admirer of St. Thomas; and this will seem the more intelligible when we fix our gaze upon Eckhart's whole manner of conceiving things. He believed himself to be as completely in harmony with the teachings of the Christian Church as he assumed a like agreement on the part

of St. Thomas. Eckhart had neither the desire to take anything away from the content of Christianity, nor the wish to add anything to it; but he desired to bring forward this content anew in his own way. It forms no part of the spiritual needs of a personality such as he was to set up new truths of this or the other kind in the place of old ones. Such a personality has grown completely intertwined with the content which it has received from tradition; but it craves to give to this content a new form, a new life.

Eckhart desired, without doubt, to remain an orthodox Christian <note>In this context, "orthodox" refers to adhering to the authorized and established doctrines of the Catholic Church at the time.</note>. The Christian truths were his own; only he desired to regard these truths in another way from that, for instance, in which St. Thomas Aquinas <note>The influential 13th-century philosopher who created a massive system of thought combining reason with faith.</note> had done. St. Thomas accepted two sources of know—

knowledge: Revelation, in matters of faith, and Reason, in those of research. Reason recognises the laws of things, that is, the spiritual in nature. Reason can raise itself above nature and grasp in the spirit from one side the Divine Being underlying nature. But it does not attain in this way to merging itself in the full being of God. <note>Aquinas taught that while human logic can prove God exists, it cannot fully "experience" or join with God without divine help.</note> A still higher truth-content must come to meet it. That is given in the Holy Scripture, which reveals what man cannot attain to through himself. The truth-content of the Scripture must be accepted by man; Reason can defend it, Reason can seek to understand it as well as possible through its powers of knowing; but never can Reason engender <note>In this context, to "engender" means to create or produce from within oneself.</note> that truth from within the spirit of man. Not what the spirit perceives is the highest truth, but what has come to this spirit from without.

<margin>The inner man</margin>

> “Here you must know that the
> Masters <note>original: "die Meister." Eckhart typically refers to the great academic and theological authorities of the past, such as Aristotle or Thomas Aquinas.</note> say that in every person there
> are two kinds of man: the one is called
> the outer man, and yet he acts through

> called the inner man, that is, that which
> is within the person. Now you must

> makes no more use of the powers of
> the soul in the outer man than so far as
> the five senses absolutely require; and
> that which is within turns not itself to
> the five senses, except in so far as it is the
> guide and conductor of the five senses, and
> shepherds them, so that they follow not
> after their craving for bestiality <note>In mystical literature, "bestiality" does not refer to a specific crime, but to the state of living purely by animal instincts and sensory pleasures without spiritual direction.</note>.” 

One
who speaks in such wise of the inner man
can no longer direct his gaze upon a Being
<term>Being: original German "Wesen," referring to the fundamental essence or ultimate reality of a thing.</term>
of things lying outside himself; for he sees
clearly that from no kind or species of the
outer world can this Being come to him.

<summary>Meister Eckhart distinguishes between the "outer man," who interacts with the physical world through the senses, and the "inner man," who must guide the senses toward God to avoid falling into a purely animal existence. The passage emphasizes that true essence or "Being" cannot be found in the external world but must be discovered within.</summary>

# MEISTER ECKHART

Color, as a physical process of the outer world, sets up a physical process in my eye and brain. Thereby I perceive color. But in this manner I can perceive of color only so much as is physical and sensuous. <term>Sense-perception</term> <note>The act of gathering information through the five senses (sight, sound, etc.), which the author argues is limited to the material surface of things.</note> cuts out everything non-sensuous from objects. Objects are thus by <term>sense-perception</term> stripped of everything about them which is non-sensuous. If I then advance to the <term>spiritual</term>, the <term>ideal content</term> <note>The conceptual or essential meaning of a thing that exists beyond its physical appearance.</note>, I in fact only reinstate in the objects what <term>sense-perception</term> has shut out therefrom. Thus <term>sense-perception</term> does not exhibit to me the deepest <term>Being</term> <note>The fundamental essence or reality of a thing.</note> of objects; it rather separates me from that being. But the <term>spiritual</term>, the <term>ideal conception</term>, seizing upon them again, unites me with that being. It shows me that objects are inwardly of exactly the same <term>spiritual</term> (<term>spiritual</term> <note>original: "geistigen"—this term encompasses both "spiritual" and "mental" in German, suggesting that the mind and the spirit are one in this context.</note>) nature as I myself.

<summary>This page explores how human thought completes our understanding of the world by restoring the spiritual essence of objects that sensory perception inherently filters out. It concludes that the inner nature of the world is identical to the spiritual nature of the human observer.</summary>

The barrier between myself and the outer world falls through this <term>spiritual conception</term> <note>A way of understanding reality that looks past physical surfaces to find the underlying "spirit" or logic of a thing.</note> of things. I am separated from the external world in so far as I am a thing of the <term>senses</term> <note>The physical tools—eyes, ears, skin—that perceive only the material world.</note> among other things of the senses. Colour and my eye are two different entities. My brain and a plant are two different things. But the <term>ideal content</term> <note>The conceptual essence or "thought-truth" of an object that the mind recognizes.</note> of the plant and of colour belong together with the ideal content of my brain and eye alike to a single ideal entity.

This way of looking at things must not be confused with the very widespread <term>anthropomorphising</term> <note>The tendency to project human emotions, intentions, or "soul" onto non-human objects, like a "angry" storm or a "happy" flower.</note> conception of the world, which imagines that it grasps the objects of the outer world by ascribing to them qualities of a <term>psychical nature</term> <note>Qualities related to the human soul or mind (from the Greek 'psyche').</note>, which are supposed to resemble the qualities of the human soul. world—namely, my own. Therefore, I can conceive of the inner world of other beings only as resembling my own inner world. Along this line of argument, one arrives at a sort of universal **ensouling** <note>The belief that all natural objects, including those we consider "inanimate," possess a soul or consciousness.</note> of all nature, a concept known as **Pan-psychism** <note>From the Greek words 'pan' (all) and 'psyche' (soul); the philosophical view that mind or a soul-like quality is a fundamental and universal feature of all things.</note>.

This view depends, however, on a failure to recognize what the awakened **inner sense** <note>A metaphorical "eye" or faculty that allows a person to perceive spiritual realities that are not visible to the physical senses.</note> really gives us. The **spiritual** <note>original: "geistig"; this term can mean both "intellectual" and "spiritual," but here it refers to the non-physical essence of things.</note> content of an external object, which reveals itself to me in my inner self, is not anything added in or by thought to the outer perception. It is just as little "added" as is the spirit of another man when we interact with him. I perceive this spiritual content through the inner sense in exactly the same way as I perceive its physical content through the external senses. And what I call my "inner life" in the sense used above (that is, my personal thoughts, feelings, and so on), is not at all, in the higher sense, my **spirit** <note>original: "Geist"; the author distinguishes between the "soul" (personal emotions and thoughts) and the "spirit" (a more universal, objective reality).</note>.

<summary>The author critiques Pan-psychism, arguing that a true "inner sense" does not project human feelings onto nature, but rather perceives the objective spiritual reality of objects directly.</summary>

# MEISTER ECKHART

Eckhart is thus conscious that it belongs to the perfectness of the Root-Being <term>Root-Being: likely a translation of the German *Urwesen*, referring to the primordial, fundamental essence from which all reality originates.</term> of the world to find Itself in the human soul. This Root-Being indeed would be imperfect, incomplete, if it lacked that part of its unfoldment <note>unfoldment: the process by which the hidden potential of the Divine manifests or reveals itself in the spiritual and physical world.</note> which comes to light in the soul. What happens in man belongs to the Root-Being; and if it did not happen, then the Root-Being would be but a part of Itself. In this sense, man can feel himself as a necessary part of the Being of the universe. This Eckhart expresses by describing his feelings towards God as follows:

> “I thank not God that He loveth me, for He may not do otherwise; whether He will it or no, His nature yet compelleth Him. . . . Therefore will I not pray to God to give me anything, nor will I praise Him for that which He hath given me. . . .” <note>Eckhart’s provocative statement emphasizes that because God is love by nature, His love is a necessity rather than a personal favor. To Eckhart, thanking God for this would be as redundant as thanking fire for being hot.</note>

# MYSTICS OF THE RENAISSANCE

Root-Being <term>Root-Being: likely a translation of the German *Urwesen*, referring to the primordial, fundamental essence from which all reality originates.</term> must not be conceived of as if the soul in its individual nature were declared to be identical with this Root-Being. The soul which is entangled in the sense-world, and so in the finite, has as such not yet got within itself the content of the Root-Being. The soul must first develop that content within itself. It must annihilate itself as an isolated being; and Meister Eckhart most aptly characterises this annihilation as *un-becoming* <note>original: "Entwerdung"; a central concept in Eckhart's mysticism referring to the process where the soul lets go of its self-centered ego and "creaturely" identity to become one with God.</note> (un-becoming or involution).

> “When I come to the root of the God-head <note>original: "Gottheit"; in Eckhart's thought, the "Godhead" is the absolute, undifferentiated essence of the Divine, which exists even "beyond" the personal God of the Trinity.</note>, none ask me whence I come and where I have been, and none doth miss me, for here there is an *un-becoming*.”

Again, the following phrase speaks very clearly about this relation: “I take a cup of water and lay therein a mirror and set it under the disc of the sun. The sun casts out its shining light on the mirror <note>Eckhart uses this analogy to show how the soul (the mirror) must be placed in the right condition (the water/purity) to reflect the Divine light (the sun), illustrating that while the reflection is of the sun, the mirror must be prepared to receive it.</note>

“His being may not become our being, but it shall be our life.” We are not to understand an already existing life—a <term>Wesung</term> <note>Middle High German: *Wesung* refers to an active, essential being or "essence-ing." The spiritual and the ideal must be experienced by the beholder as vividly as individual human nature experiences ordinary daily life.

From these starting points, Meister Eckhart also builds a pure concept of Freedom. In its ordinary, worldly life, the soul is not free; it is interwoven with a web of "lower causes" and simply carries out what those causes demand. However, through "beholding" or mystical vision, the soul is lifted out of the domain of these external causes. It no longer acts as a separate, isolated individual soul. The very root of being is laid bare within the soul, and that root cannot be moved to action by anything except itself. “God does not compel the

**Vocabulary from this section:**
*   **Natural being:** Man as a biological entity.
*   **Moral life:** The pursuit of ethics and spiritual purpose.
*   **Vision of God:** The internal realization or contemplation of the divine (*Gottesanschauung*).
*   **Spirituality:** The realm of the mind and soul (*Geistigkeit*).
*   **Creature of nature:** A being bound by physical and biological laws.
*   **Natural creation:** The entirety of the physical world.
*   **Inner life:** The subjective, spiritual experience of the individual.

# MYSTICS OF THE RENAISSANCE

If he calls only this nature Being or “that which is,” then he must be able to reach out to the vision which recognises as the higher, Non-being, or “that which is not.” <note>In mystical traditions, "Non-being" does not mean "nothingness." It refers to a state of existence so far beyond physical reality that human language—which is designed to describe physical things—cannot find a word for it. Therefore, it is called "not a thing" or "Non-being."</note> Tauler seeks for no God who is present in the same sense as a natural force; he seeks no God who has created the world in the sense of human creation. In him lives the clear insight that the conception of creation even of the Fathers of the Church <note>The "Fathers of the Church" refers to the foundational Christian theologians of the first few centuries AD, such as Augustine or Jerome, whose works shaped medieval thought.</note> is only idealised human creating. It is clear to him that God is not to be found as nature’s working and her laws are found, by science. Tauler is well aware that we must not add in thought anything to nature as God. He knows that whoever thinks God, in his sense, no longer thinks thought-content, as does one who has grasped nature in thought. Therefore, Tauler seeks not to think God, but to

<summary>Tauler distinguishes God from the physical laws of nature, arguing that the divine cannot be understood through scientific observation or by imagining God as a human-like creator. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Johannes Tauler’s mystical philosophy, specifically the transition from seeking to "think" about God to actually "thinking divinely." # MYSTICS OF THE RENAISSANCE

->THE FRIENDSHIP OF GOD<-

think divinely, to think as God thinks. <note>Tauler is suggesting that the goal of the mystic is not to study God as an external object, but to align one's own mind with the divine perspective.</note> The knowledge of nature is not enriched by the knowledge of God, but *transformed*. The knower of God does not know a different thing from the knower of nature, but he knows in a different way. Not one single letter can the knower of God add to the knowledge of nature; but through his whole knowing of nature there shines a new light.

What root-feelings will take possession of a man's soul who contemplates the world from this point of view, will depend upon how he regards that experience of the soul which brings about spiritual re-birth. <note>"Spiritual re-birth" in this context refers to a profound internal shift where a person stops identifying solely with their physical senses and begins to identify with a deeper, spiritual reality.</note> Within this experience, man is wholly a natural being, when he considers himself in his interaction with the rest of nature; and he is wholly a spiritual being when he considers the conditions into which this re-birth has

# THE FRIENDSHIP OF GOD

"...the blissful divine word, which is the second person in the Trinity <note>The Christian doctrine of God existing as three persons: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. Tauler is referring to the Son, or the 'Word' (Logos).</note>, Him the Father begets <note>original: "begetteth"; to bring into being or father.</note> in the soul, . . . and because of this the soul possesses such great and special dignity."¹ The stories of scripture become for Tauler the garment in which he clothes the happiness of the inner life. "Herod <note>In the Bible, King Herod attempted to kill the infant Jesus. Tauler uses him here as a symbol for the distractions and temptations of the material world.</note>, who drove out the child and sought to slay him, is a likeness of the world, which still seeks to kill this child in a person of faith; therefore one should and must flee from the world if we desire to keep that child alive in us—but that child is the enlightened, believing soul of each and every person."

As Tauler directs his gaze mainly upon the "natural man" <note>The human being driven by physical senses and worldly desires, before undergoing a spiritual awakening.</note>, he is comparatively less concerned with telling us exactly what happens when the "higher man" <note>The spiritual self that is conscious of its connection to the divine.</note> enters into the natural man.

this teaching has not yet awakened to full life in him. He thinks it with his understanding <term>understanding: Used here to denote the German *Verstand*, the analytical or conceptual faculty of the mind, which differentiates and categorizes information without necessarily experiencing its essence.</term>: but he does not yet live in it with every fibre of his personality. He is thus teaching about a state of things which he has not yet completely lived through in himself. The description of the condition corresponds to the truth; yet this truth has no value if it does not gain life, if it does not bring itself forth in reality as actually existent.

In man also this must be so. He gives himself a meaning in reality; he does not halt and stand still at the meaning he already has, which his contemplation shows him. He does not aim to be the knower of the hidden, or of the self-revealing existent God, but to be the **friend of the divine doing and working**, <note>This "friendship" suggests an active partnership or co-creation between the human and the divine, rather than a relationship of a servant to a master.</note> which is exalted over both being and non-being.

# THE FRIENDSHIP OF GOD

The layman, who came to the Master <note>The "Master" refers to the famous 14th-century Dominican preacher Johannes Tauler, while the "layman" is a mysterious figure often called the "Friend of God from the Oberland."</note>, was a <term>Friend of God</term> <note>The "Friends of God" (Gottesfreunde) were a medieval movement of pious individuals who sought a direct, personal relationship with God, often bypassing formal church hierarchy.</note> in this sense, and through him the Master became—from a contemplator of the being of God—one who is **"alive in the spirit,"** one who not only contemplated, but lived in the higher sense. 

The Master now no longer brought forth concepts and ideas of the understanding from his inner nature, but these concepts and ideas burst forth from him as living, **actualised spirit**. He no longer merely edified his hearers; he shook the very foundations of their being. He no longer plunged their souls into their inner being; he led them into a new life. This is recounted to us symbolically: about forty people fell down through his preaching and lay as if dead. <note>This dramatic account is found in the "History of the Master Tauler," where his first sermon after a period of silent retreat was so spiritually potent that it caused a physical collapse in his audience, symbolizing a "death" of the old self and a rebirth into the spirit.</note>

->MYSTICS OF THE RENAISSANCE<-

<term>Perfection</term> <note>In mystical theology, "Perfection" refers to the absolute, undivided nature of God.</note>. "When the <term>creature</term> <note>Any created being, specifically a human soul in this context.</note> assumes to itself somewhat of good, as <term>Being</term>, <term>Life</term>, <term>Knowledge</term>, <term>Power</term>—in short, aught of that which one calls good—and thinks that it *is* that, or that it belongs to it or comes from it, so often and so much as that happens, does the creature turn away." 

"The created soul of man has two eyes. The one is the possibility of seeing in <term>eternity</term> <note>The "eye of the spirit," which looks toward the timeless, unchanging divine reality.</note>; the other of seeing in time and in <term>creation</term> <note>The "eye of the senses," which focuses on the material world and individual objects.</note>." 

"Man should therefore stand and be quite free without himself, that is without <term>self-ness</term>, <term>I-ness</term>, 'me,' and 'mine,' for me and the like, so that he as little seeks and thinks of himself and what is his in all things as if it did not exist; and he should therefore also think little of himself, as if he were not, and as if another had done all his deeds."¹

<margin>The soul must be emptied of "me" and "mine" to see with the eye of eternity.</margin>

¹ Chapter 15, <insert>*The Book of the Man from Frankfurt*</insert> <note>Also known as the *Theologia Germanica*, a handbook for a mystical way of life that influenced Martin Luther.</note>.

<summary>The text explains that spiritual fall occurs when a person claims divine qualities like "Life" or "Power" as their own property. It describes the human soul as having two visions—one for the eternal and one for the temporal—and mandates a total release of the ego to achieve true freedom.</summary>

->119<-

One must also take account of the fact, regarding the writer of these sentences, that the <term>thought-content</term> <note>The specific religious ideas, doctrines, or "raw material" used to build a philosophy.</note> to which he gives a direction through his higher ideas and feelings, is that of a believing priest in the spirit of his own time. We are concerned here not with the specific thought-content, but with the *direction*; not with the thoughts themselves, but with the *way of thinking*. 

Anyone who does not live within <term>Christian dogmas</term> <note>The established, authoritative teachings and articles of faith of the Church.</note> as he does, but instead lives within the concepts of <term>natural science</term>, will find different thoughts in these sentences. However, even with these different thoughts, the author points in the same direction. 

And this direction is the one that leads to the overcoming of <term>self-hood</term> <note>The narrow, ego-driven sense of "I" that feels separate from the rest of the world; the "small self."</note> by the Self-hood itself. The highest light shines for man in his <term>Ego</term> <note>The core "I" or individual consciousness.</note>. Yet, this light only then imparts the correct reflection to his <term>concept-world</term> <note>The internal mental framework or worldview through which a person understands and interprets reality.</note> when he becomes aware that...

<summary>The text argues that the "direction" of a mystic's thought—the movement toward overcoming the ego—is more universal than the specific religious or scientific language used to describe it. It suggests that while the individual "I" is the source of spiritual light, it must be transformed to see the world correctly.</summary>

## CARDINAL NICHOLAS OF CUSA

A GLORIOUSLY shining star in the sky of the thought-life of the Middle Ages is <term>Nicholas Chrysippus of Cusa</term> <note>Nicholas of Cusa (1401–1464), also known as Nicolaus Cusanus. The name "Chrysippus" likely refers to his intellectual stature, comparing him to the ancient Stoic philosopher of that name.</note> (at <term>Trevis</term> <note>A reference to Trier, the region in modern-day Germany where he was born in the town of Kues (Cusa).</note>, 1401-1464). He stands upon the summit of the knowledge of his time. In mathematics he accomplished remarkable work. In natural science he may be described as the forerunner of <term>Copernicus</term> <note>Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543), the astronomer who proposed that the Earth revolves around the Sun.</note>, for he took up the standpoint that the earth is a moving celestial body like others. The Scholastic could not attain to any other conception of knowledge, for the dogmatic content of his theology prevented his doing so. If he had directed the gaze of his <term>spiritual eye</term> <note>A metaphor for the intuitive or intellectual capacity to perceive spiritual truths beyond physical sight.</note> upon that which he regards as an image only, he would then have seen that the spiritual content of things reveals itself in this supposed image; he would then have found that in his own inner being, God does not alone image Himself, but that He lives therein, is present there in His own nature. He would have beheld in gazing into his own inner being, not a dark abyss, an endless emptiness, but also not merely an image of God; he would have felt that a life pulses within him, which is the very life of God itself; and that his own life is verily just God’s life.

This the Scholastic dared not admit.

### CARDINAL NICHOLAS OF CUSA

<term>Nicholas of Cusa</term> <note>Nicholas of Cusa (1401–1464) was a German philosopher, theologian, and mathematician who sought to bridge the gap between human logic and the infinite nature of God through his concept of "learned ignorance."</note> sets out to mount from the knowledge one acquires in the isolated sciences up to the inner living experiences. There can be no doubt that the excellent logical technique which the <term>Scholastics</term> <note>The medieval academics who used formal logic to defend and organize Church doctrine, often prioritizing intellectual rules over personal mystical experience.</note> have developed, and for which Nicholas himself was educated, forms a most effective means of attaining to these inner experiences, even though the Scholastics themselves were held back from this road by their <term>positive faith</term> <note>In this context, "positive faith" refers to established religious doctrines accepted as absolute truth based on external authority or revelation, rather than personal insight.</note>.

But one can only understand Nicholas fully when one reflects that his calling as a priest, which raised him to the dignity of Cardinal, prevented him from coming to a complete breach with the faith of the Church, which found an expression

<summary>This page introduces Nicholas of Cusa, explaining how he used the logical tools of the Scholastics to pursue personal mystical experience while remaining bound by his official role in the Church.</summary>

## MYSTICS OF THE RENAISSANCE

appropriate to the age in <term>Scholasticism</term> <note>The dominant philosophical and theological system of the Middle Ages, which sought to bridge faith and reason using formal logic.</note>. We find him so far along the road, that a single step further would necessarily have carried him out of the Church. We shall therefore understand the Cardinal best if we complete the one step more which he did not take; and then, looking backwards, throw light upon what he aimed at.

The most significant thought in Nicholas’s mental life is that of “**learned ignorance**.” <note>Original Latin: *docta ignorantia*. This is the central concept of Nicholas's philosophy, suggesting that true wisdom lies in recognizing the limitations of human reason when trying to understand the infinite nature of God.</note> By this he means a form of knowing which occupies a higher level as compared with ordinary knowledge. In the lower sense, knowledge is the grasping of an object by the mind, or spirit. The most important characteristic of knowing is that it gives us light about something outside of the spirit, that therefore it directs its gaze upon something different from itself. The

<summary>This page defines Nicholas of Cusa’s core philosophy of "learned ignorance," positioning his thought as a near-departure from traditional Church Scholasticism toward a higher form of spiritual cognition.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of Nicholas of Cusa’s philosophy, picking up from the previous page's introduction of "learned ignorance." It explains how the spirit moves from observing external objects to recognizing its own nature within the "being" of things.</meta>

# CARDINAL NICHOLAS OF CUSA

spirit, therefore, is concerned in the **knowing-process** with things thought of as outside itself. Now what the spirit develops in itself about things is the **being** of those things. The things are spirit. Man sees the spirit so far only through the **sensible encasement** <note>The outward, material form of objects that we perceive with our physical senses.</note>. What lies outside the spirit is only this sensible encasement; the **being** of the things enters into the spirit. If, then, the spirit turns its attention to this being of the things, which is of like nature with itself, then it can no longer talk of knowing; for it is not looking at anything outside of itself, but is looking at some-thing which is part of itself; is, indeed, looking at itself. It no longer knows; it only looks upon itself. It is no longer concerned with a "knowing," but with a "**not-knowing**" <note>Original Latin: *ignorantia*. In Nicholas’s "learned ignorance," this "not-knowing" is a higher state where the mind transcends logical definitions to reach a mystical unity with the subject.</note>. No longer does man "**grasp**" <note>To "grasp" (or *begreifen* in the original context) implies a conceptual limitation; Nicholas suggests the divine is beyond such mental capture.</note> something through the mind;

<summary>This page describes the transition from external sensory knowledge to internal spiritual realization, where the mind moves beyond "knowing" objects to a state of "not-knowing" that reflects its own essence.</summary>

he "beholds without conceiving" <note>In this context, to "behold without conceiving" means to experience a truth directly with the spirit without trying to limit it with definitions or logical categories.</note> his own life. This highest stage of knowing is, in comparison with the lower stages, a "not-knowing." <note>Original Latin: *ignorantia*. For Nicholas, this is not a lack of intelligence, but a state where the mind acknowledges that the infinite nature of God and life cannot be fully "known" by finite logic.</note>

But it is obvious that the essential being of things can only be reached through this stage of knowing. Thus Nicholas of Cusa in speaking of his "**learned not-knowing**" <note>Original Latin: *docta ignorantia*. This is his most famous concept, suggesting that the most profound wisdom comes from a humble awareness of the limits of human reason.</note> is really speaking of nothing else but "knowing" come to a new birth, as an inner experience. He tells us himself how he came to this inner experience. "I made many efforts to unite the ideas of God and the world, of Christ and the Church, into a single root-idea; but nothing satisfied me until at last, on my way back from Greece by sea, my mind's vision, as if by an illumination from above, soared up to that perception in which God appeared to me as the supreme **Unity of all contradictions**." <note>Original Latin: *coincidentia oppositorum*. This describes the mystical idea that in the absolute nature of the Divine, all things that seem like opposites to our human minds—such as the finite and the infinite, or beginning and end—exist together in harmony.</note>

<summary>Nicholas of Cusa describes his breakthrough discovery of the "Unity of Contradictions," an inner spiritual illumination he experienced during a sea voyage that allowed him to see God as the reconciliation of all opposing ideas.</summary>

This "learned not-knowing" <note>original: *docta ignorantia*. This is Cusa’s central philosophy: the realization that the finite human mind cannot truly "know" the infinite God, and thus true wisdom lies in recognizing the limits of our knowledge.</note> is, in a certain sense, already prefigured in these earlier writings. Here, we can only point out the essential characteristic of the way of perceiving things found in these works.

Humanity primarily knows the objects of the sense-world. We form thoughts about the nature and actions of these things. However, the Primal Cause <note>The ultimate source or origin of all existence.</note> of all things must exist at a higher level than the things themselves. Therefore, man must not attempt to grasp this Primal Cause by using the same concepts and ideas he uses for material things. If he attributes characteristics to the Root-Being <note>original: *Urwesen*. This term refers to the fundamental essence or "Godhead" that underlies all of reality.</note> (God) that he has learned from observing lower things, such attributes can at best be only helpful aids for his limited spirit, which drags the Root-Being down to its own level in order to conceive of it.

### CARDINAL NICHOLAS OF CUSA

In truth, therefore, no attribute whatsoever which lower things possess can be predicated of God. It must not even be said that God "is." For "being" too is a concept which man has formed from lower things. But God is exalted above "being" and "not-being." <note>This is the core of "negative theology"—the idea that God is so far beyond human comprehension that we can only say what God is *not*, rather than what God *is*.</note> The God to whom we ascribe attributes, is therefore not the true God. We come to the true God, when we think of an "Over-God" <note>original: *Über-Gott*. This term describes a divinity that transcends the very category of "God" as understood by human language or religion.</note> above and beyond any God with such attributes. Of this "Over-God" we can know nothing in the ordinary sense. In order to attain to Him, "knowing" must merge into "not-knowing."

One sees that at the root of such a view there lies the consciousness that man himself is able to develop a higher knowing, which is no longer mere knowing—in a purely natural manner—on the basis of what his various sciences have yielded <note>Cusa suggests that while science and logic are useful for understanding the physical world, they reach a limit when facing the infinite; at that point, a different kind of spiritual awareness must take over.</note>

### Key Concepts

*   **Nicholas of Cusa:** A 15th-century cardinal and philosopher who bridged the gap between medieval faith and Renaissance science.
*   **Christianity:** The religious framework that deeply informed Nicholas's worldview.
*   **Not-knowing:** The philosophical state where one realizes the limits of rational thought.
*   **Spiritual life:** The internal development of human consciousness and its relation to the divine.
*   **Science:** The systematic study of the world which, according to the author, can create a sense of separation from nature.
*   **Nature:** The physical world and the "chain of workings" that humans belong to.

Steiner views him as a key figure who struggled with the limits of human reason.</note> Nicholas of Cusa, he will believe that he is travelling his own road; and yet in reality he will be only following the path which his own spiritual evolution has pointed out for him.

Now there are—in the main—three roads which one can follow, when once one has reached the point at which Nicholas had arrived: the one is <term>**positive faith**<gloss>Steiner uses this term to describe "received" religion—truths accepted from an external authority or scripture rather than discovered through one's own inner cognitive experience.</gloss></term>, forcing itself upon us from without; the second is despair; one stands alone with one’s burden, and feels the <note>Steiner is describing an existential crisis where the subjective "mental world" feels so disconnected from reality that the universe itself seems to lose its solid foundation.</note> whole universe tottering with oneself; the third road is the development of the deepest, most inward powers of man. Confidence, trust in the world must be one of our guides upon this third path; courage, to follow that confidence whithersoever it may lead us, must be the other.

<summary>Steiner outlines three responses to the isolation of the intellect: retreating into external religious dogma, falling into existential despair, or courageously developing one's own inner spiritual powers to find a new connection to the world.</summary>

<keywords>Nicholas of Cusa, Godhead, spiritual evolution, positive faith, despair, inward powers, existential crisis, mystical path</keywords>

## AGRIPPA VON NETTESHEIM AND THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS

Both <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (an occult philosopher) and Paracelsus (a famous physician and alchemist) were key Renaissance figures who sought to bridge the gap between medieval mysticism and the emerging scientific observation of the world.</note> **Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa von Nettesheim** (1487-1535) and **Theophrastus Paracelsus** (1493-1541) followed the same road along which points Nicholas of Cusa's way of conceiving things. They devoted themselves to the study of <term>**Nature**<gloss>In this context, Nature is viewed not as a machine, but as a living, spiritual organism that reveals the "thoughts" of the Divine through its physical forms.</gloss></term>, and sought to discover her laws by all the means in their power and as thoroughly as possible. In this knowledge of Nature, they saw the true basis of all <term>**higher knowledge**<gloss>For Steiner, "higher knowledge" refers to a direct, cognitive perception of the spiritual world that goes beyond what the five senses can provide.</gloss></term>. They strove to develop this higher knowledge from within the science or knowledge of Nature by bringing that knowledge to a <term>**new birth in the spirit**<gloss>This is a central mystical concept where the human mind does not just observe facts, but undergoes a transformation so that the spiritual essence of the world can "speak" through the individual's own consciousness.</gloss></term>.

<summary>Steiner introduces Agrippa von Nettesheim and Paracelsus as successors to Nicholas of Cusa, highlighting their method of using the rigorous study of the physical natural world as a foundation for spiritual enlightenment.</summary>

<keywords>Agrippa von Nettesheim, Theophrastus Paracelsus, Nicholas of Cusa, Nature, higher knowledge, spirit, Renaissance philosophy, spiritual rebirth</keywords>

## NETTESHEIM AND PARACELSUS

**Agrippa von Nettesheim** led a much varied life. He sprang from a noble family and was born in Cologne. He early studied medicine and law, and sought to obtain clear insight into the processes of **Nature** in the way which was then customary within certain circles and societies, or even among isolated investigators, who studiously kept secret whatever of the knowledge of Nature they discovered. <note>In the Renaissance, many scientific and alchemical discoveries were kept "hermetic" or secret, partly to protect the researcher from charges of heresy by the Church and partly because knowledge was viewed as a sacred power intended only for the "initiated."</note> For these purposes he went repeatedly to Paris, to Italy, and to England, and also visited the famous **Abbot Trithemius of Sponheim** <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516) was a brilliant scholar and cryptographer who was often accused of practicing magic because of his advanced knowledge of nature and communication.</note> in Würzburg. He taught at various times in learned institutions, and here and there entered the service of rich and distinguished people, at whose disposal he placed his abilities as a statesman and a man of science. If the services that he rendered are not always described by his <note>The text breaks off here; Agrippa's reputation was often polarized between that of a brilliant scholar and a dangerous sorcerer.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the early life and education of Agrippa von Nettesheim, focusing on his travels across Europe and his involvement in the secretive scientific and "occult" circles of the Renaissance, including his mentorship under Abbot Trithemius.</summary>

The "spirit in Nature" exists only for spirit. So surely as Nature in this sense is spiritual, so surely too is there nothing in Nature, of all that is perceived by my bodily organs, which is immediately spiritual. There exists nothing spiritual which can appear to my eye as spiritual.

Therefore, I must not seek for the spirit as such in Nature; but that is what I am doing when I interpret any occurrence in the external world immediately as spiritual; when, for instance, I ascribe to a plant a soul which is supposed to be only remotely analogous to that of man. He shrinks from nothing, in order to learn to know Nature and her workings in all directions. For this purpose he made journeys to Sweden, Hungary, Spain, Portugal, and the East.

Naturally I have not the least intention of blurring the difference that exists between a scientist of the sixteenth and one of the nineteenth century. It was, indeed, this latter century which for the first time was able to see, in the full scientific sense, the phenomena of living beings in such a connection that their natural relationship and actual descent, right up to man, stood out clearly before one's eyes. ->217<-

->NETTESHEIM AND PARACELSUS<-

Thereby he was enabled truly to ascribe to man a **self-creative activity**. For if the divine root of being is, as it were, given once for all, then there can be no question of any truly creative activity in man. It is not man, living in time, who then creates, but it is God, who is from **Eternity**, that creates. But for Paracelsus there is no such God from Eternity. <note>Paracelsus moves away from the traditional view of God as a finished, external creator, instead seeing the divine as a force active within time itself.</note> For him there is only an **eternal happening**, and man is one link in this eternal happening. What man forms, was previously in no sense existent. What man creates, is, as he creates it, a new, original creation. If it is to be called divine, it can only be so-called in the sense in which it is a human creation. Therefore Paracelsus can assign to man a role in the building of the universe, which makes him a **co-architect** in its creation. The <term>divine root of being</term> <note>Referring back to the primordial essence (often termed "Urwesen" in the original German) from which all life and spirit emerge.</note> is

## VALENTINE WEIGEL AND JACOB BOEHME

In the view of Paracelsus, what mattered most was to acquire ideas about Nature which should breathe the spirit of the <term>higher insight</term> <note>The "higher insight" refers to a way of seeing the world where the observer recognizes the divine or spiritual essence within material things.</note> that he represented. A thinker related to him, who applied the same mode of conceiving things to his own nature especially, is **VALENTINE WEIGEL** (1533-1588) <note>Weigel was a German pastor and mystic whose writings were mostly published after his death to avoid persecution by orthodox authorities.</note>. He grew up out of **Protestant theology** in a like sense to that in which <term>Eckhart, Tauler, and Suso</term> <note>Meister Eckhart, Johannes Tauler, and Henry Suso were the three most prominent figures of late-medieval German mysticism, emphasizing the "birth of God in the soul."</note> grew up out of **Roman Catholic theology**. He has predecessors in <term>Sebastian Frank</term> <note>A humanist and radical reformer (1499–1542) who believed that the true "Word of God" was internal and spiritual rather than found in the literal text of the Bible.</note> and <term>Caspar Schwenckfeldt</term> <note>A Silesian nobleman and reformer (1489–1561) who emphasized the "Inner Word" and spiritual transformation over external rituals and church organization.</note>. These two, as contrasted with the orthodox Churchmen clinging to <term>external profession</term> <note>The public declaration of faith and adherence to established church rituals and doctrines, which the mystics felt lacked true internal substance.</note>,

<summary>This page introduces Valentine Weigel as a successor to Paracelsus’s thought, situating him within a tradition of Protestant mystics who sought internal spiritual truth over rigid church doctrine. It draws a historical parallel between these Protestant thinkers and earlier Catholic mystics like Meister Eckhart.</summary>

<keywords>Valentine Weigel, Jacob Boehme, Paracelsus, Protestant theology, Meister Eckhart, Johannes Tauler, Henry Suso, Sebastian Frank, Caspar Schwenckfeldt, mysticism, internal faith, Reformation</keywords>

pointed downward toward the deepening of the <term>inner life</term> <note>The "inner life" refers to the spiritual core of a person, which these mystics believed was the only true place where one could encounter the divine, rather than through external rituals.</note>. For them it is not that Jesus whom the Gospels preach who is of value, but the **Christ** who can be born in every man as his deeper nature, and become for him the Saviour from the lower life and the guide to ideal uplifting. <note>This concept, often called the "Internal Word" or "Christ within," suggests that the historical life of Jesus is a blueprint for a spiritual process that must happen inside every individual believer.</note>

Weigel feels himself driven to gain a clear understanding of his relation to the teaching of the Church; and that leads him on further to investigate the basic foundations of all knowledge. ->231<-

->WEIGEL AND BOEHME<-

"**Light of Mercy.**" <note>original: "Gnadenlicht." In Weigel’s theology, this is the divine illumination that allows a person to perceive spiritual truths, contrasting with the "light of nature" used for worldly understanding.</note> This "**Light of Mercy**" is, in reality, nothing other than the **self-knowledge** of the spirit in man, or the **re-birth of knowledge** on the higher level of **beholding** <note>original: "Anschauung"; a term for direct, intuitive spiritual vision where one "sees" truth without the need for logical deduction.</note>.

Now just as **Nicholas of Cusa** <note>A 15th-century German philosopher and cardinal who argued that the human mind can approach God through "learned ignorance" and the "coincidence of opposites."</note>, in following up his road from **knowing** to **beholding**, does not really bring about the **re-birth** of the knowledge he has gained, on the higher level, but only the faith of the Church in which he was brought up appears deceptively before him as such a **re-birth**, so is it also the case with **Weigel**. He guides himself to the right road, but loses it again in the very moment in which he steps upon it. He who will travel the road that **Weigel** points out, can regard the latter as his guide only as far as the starting-point.

<summary>The author identifies the "Light of Mercy" as the spiritual self-knowledge gained through direct intuition. He critiques Nicholas of Cusa and Valentin Weigel, arguing that while they found the path to spiritual "beholding," they both retreated into traditional church dogma rather than following their insights to their logical conclusion.</summary>

What rings out to meet us from the works of the Master-Shoemaker of **Görlitz** <note>A town in Upper Lusatia (modern-day Germany/Poland border) where Boehme lived. He lives as a **spiritual anchorite**, <term>spiritual anchorite: Traditionally a person living in total religious seclusion. The purposeless <note>In this context, the "purposeless" refers to actions or natural events that seem to lack a divine goal or moral direction, which Boehme equates with evil.</note> seems to him an evil something in Nature, just as evil seems to him something purposeless in man. Similar fundamental forces rule both here and there. To one who has known the origin of evil in man, the source of evil in Nature also lies open and clear.

Now, how can the evil as well as the good flow forth from the very same <term>Root-Being: Referred to in German as the *Urwesen*, this is the primordial, divine essence from which all of creation is born.</term>? members, so also the <term>Root-Being: Referred to in German as the *Urwesen*, this is the primordial, divine essence from which all of creation is born.</term>. And as human life is poured out into this multiplicity <note>The "multiplicity" refers to the vast variety of individual things and beings in the physical world, all originating from a single source.</note> of members, so too the Root-Being is poured out into the manifoldness of the things of this world. As true as it is that the entire man has only one life, so true is it that every member has its own life. And as little as it contradicts the whole harmonious life of a man, that his hand should turn itself against his own body and wound it, so little is it impossible that the things of the world, which live the life of the Root-Being in their own way, should turn themselves against each other. Thus the Root-Being, in dividing itself among different lives, confers upon each such life the capacity to turn itself against the whole.

It is not from the good that evil streams forth, but from the way in which the good lives. As the light is only able to shine <note>Boehme often used the analogy of light to explain how the divine essence requires a "mirror" or resistance to become manifest; without something to shine upon, light remains invisible.</note>

<summary>Boehme explains the origin of evil by comparing the divine Root-Being to a human body: just as a single body’s members have individual lives and can harm the whole, individual parts of creation can turn against the divine unity. Evil is thus presented not as a separate force, but as a consequence of how the "good" manifests in the diverse, individual things of the world.</summary>

# MYSTICS OF THE RENAISSANCE

It is with such views that Jacob Boehme <note>Jacob Boehme (1575–1624) was a German mystic and theologian whose complex system of "theosophy" attempted to explain the origin of evil and the nature of God through alchemical and natural symbols.</note> seeks to fathom that world which for him, according to the knowledge of his time, was reckoned as the actual world of fact. For him all is fact which is so regarded by the natural science of his time and by the Bible. His way of conceiving things is one thing, his world of facts quite another. One can imagine the former applied to a totally different knowledge of facts. And thus there appears before our eyes a Jacob Boehme as he might stand at the parting of the nineteenth and the twentieth centuries. Such a one would not saturate with his way of conceiving things the six days' creation <note>The "six days' creation" refers to the Hexameron, the biblical account of the world's origin found in the Book of Genesis.</note> work of the Bible and the fight of the angels and the devils, but Lyell's <note>Charles Lyell (1797–1875) was a Scottish geologist whose work established the principle of uniformitarianism—the idea that Earth was shaped by slow, consistent natural processes over vast timescales.</note> geological knowledge and the facts of Haeckel's <note>Ernst Haeckel (1834–1919) was a German biologist and philosopher who was a primary proponent of Darwinism in continental Europe.</note> *The History of Creation* <note>original title: *Natürliche Schöpfungsgeschichte* (1868)</note>. ## GIORDANO BRUNO AND ANGELUS SILESIUS

IN the first decade <note>original: "decennium"</note> of the sixteenth century, the scientific genius of Nicholas Copernicus (1473-1543) conceived <note>literally "thinks out"</note> within the castle of Heilsberg <note>located in Prussia, now Lidzbark Warmiński in modern-day Poland</note> an intellectual structure which compels the men of subsequent epochs to look up to the starry heavens with different conceptions than those which their forefathers in antiquity and the Middle Ages held. To them, the earth was their dwelling-place, remaining at rest in the center of the Universe. The stars, however, were seen by them as beings of a perfect nature, whose motion took place in circles because the circle was the representative <note>or symbol</note> of perfection.

<summary>This page describes the revolutionary shift in scientific thought initiated by Nicholas Copernicus at Heilsberg, which challenged the ancient and medieval belief in a stationary, central Earth and perfect circular celestial motion.</summary>
<keywords>Nicholas Copernicus, Heilsberg, Prussia, Heliocentrism, Scientific Revolution, Geocentrism, Giordano Bruno, Angelus Silesius</keywords>

## BRUNO AND SILESIUS

In that which the stars showed to human senses they beheld something of the nature of soul, something spiritual. It was one kind of speech that the things and processes upon earth spoke to man; quite another, that of the shining stars, beyond the moon in the pure ether <term>æther: in ancient and medieval cosmology, the "fifth element" thought to fill the universe beyond the terrestrial sphere</term>, which seemed like some spiritual nature filling space. Nicholas of Cusa <note>1401–1464, a German philosopher and cardinal who proposed that the Earth was a moving star before Copernicus</note> had already formed other ideas.

Through Copernicus, earth became for man a brother-being in face of the other heavenly bodies, a star moving like others. All the difference that earth has to show for man he could now reduce to this: that earth is his dwelling-place. He was no longer forced to think differently about the events of this earth and those of the rest of universal space. The world of his senses had expanded itself into the most remote spaces. He was
