## AFTERWORD

<margin>¹ Compare my book: *Goethe’s Worldview* <note>original: *Goethe's Weltanschauung*</note>, Weimar, 1897.</margin>

NEARLY two and a half centuries have passed since Angelus Silesius gathered up the profound wisdom of his predecessors in his *Cherubinean Wanderer* <note>Angelus Silesius (1624–1677) was a German priest and physician; his *Cherubinean Wanderer* is a seminal collection of mystical aphorisms that seek the divine within the self.</note>. These centuries have brought rich insights into Nature. Goethe opened a vast perspective to natural science. He sought to follow up the eternal, unchangeable laws of Nature's working, to that summit where, with like necessity, they cause man to come into being, just as on a lower level they bring forth the stone.¹ Lamarck, Darwin, Haeckel <note>The author connects the mystical tradition to the giants of evolutionary biology: Jean-Baptiste Lamarck, Charles Darwin, and Ernst Haeckel, suggesting a continuity between spiritual and biological development.</note>, and others, have laboured further in the direction of this way of conceiving things. The

<summary>This page begins the concluding afterword, connecting the mystical insights of Angelus Silesius to the modern scientific theories of Goethe, Darwin, and Haeckel. It argues that the same natural laws governing the physical world also necessitate the existence of human consciousness.</summary>

<keywords>Angelus Silesius, Cherubinean Wanderer, Johann Wolfgang von Goethe, natural science, Lamarck, Darwin, Ernst Haeckel, evolution, worldview, Renaissance mysticism</keywords>

->270<-

## MYSTICS OF THE RENAISSANCE

The "question of all questions," regarding the natural origin of man, found its answer in the nineteenth century; and other related problems in the realm of natural events have also found their solutions. Today, people understand that it is not necessary to step outside the realm of the actual and the perceptible in order to understand the developmental sequence of living beings, right up to man, in a purely natural manner.

And, further, J. G. Fichte's <note>Johann Gottlieb Fichte (1762–1814) was a central figure in German Idealism who argued that the "Ego" or "I" is the fundamental principle of all knowledge and reality.</note> insight has illuminated the nature of the human ego, showing the human soul where to seek itself and what it truly is.¹ Hegel <note>Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel (1770–1831) developed a vast philosophical system that attempted to explain all of history, nature, and thought as the unfolding of a single, rational "Absolute Spirit."</note> has extended the realm of thought over all the provinces of being, striving to grasp the entire perceptible world through thought.

How, then, do those men of genius whose thoughts have been traced in the preceding pages, appear in the light of a world-conception <note>A "world-conception" (often a translation of the German *Weltanschauung*) refers to a comprehensive philosophy or framework for understanding reality.</note> which takes into account the scientific achievements of the centuries that followed their epoch? They still believed in a “supernatural” story of creation. How do their thoughts appear when confronted with a “natural” history of creation, which the science of the nineteenth century has built up?

->AFTERWORD<-

self approaches it with a shallow thought-world. I feel something higher, more glorious, when I let the revelations of the “natural history of creation” <note>original: *Natürliche Schöpfungsgeschichte*; a reference to the 1868 work by Ernst Haeckel that brought Darwinian evolution to the German public.</note> work upon me, than when the supernatural miracle stories of the confessions of faith force themselves upon me. In no “holy book” do I know aught <note>anything</note> that unveils for me anything as lofty as the “sober” fact, that every human **germ** <note>In the scientific language of the 19th century, "germ" refers to the embryo or the earliest stage of a developing organism.</note> in the mother’s womb repeats in brief, one after the other, those animal types which its animal ancestors have passed through. <note>The author is referring to the "biogenetic law," the theory that an embryo's development (ontogeny) mirrors the evolutionary history of its species (phylogeny).</note> If only we fill our hearts with the glory of the facts that our senses behold, then we shall have little left over for “wonders” which do not lie in the course of Nature. If we experience the Spirit in ourselves, then we have no need of such in external Nature.
   In my ***Philosophy of Freedom***, (Berlin, 1894) <note>Steiner's central philosophical work, which argues that true freedom is found through "spiritual activity" in thinking.</note> I have described my view

->276<-

->MYSTICS OF THE RENAISSANCE<-

of the world, which has no thought of driving out the Spirit, because it beholds Nature as Darwin <note>Charles Darwin (1809–1882), the English naturalist whose theories of evolution were often seen as a threat to religion. Steiner argues here that Darwinism and spirituality are compatible.</note> and Haeckel beheld her. A plant, an animal, gains nothing for me if I people it with souls of which my senses give me no information. I do not seek in the external world for a “deeper,” “more soulful” being of things; nay, I do not even assume it, because I believe that the insight which shines forth for me in my inner being guards me against it. I believe that the things of the **sense-world** <term>sense-world: the physical reality accessible to our five senses, as opposed to a purely intellectual or "supersensible" realm.</term> are, in fact, just as they present themselves to us, because I see that a right self-knowledge leads us to this: that in Nature we should seek nothing but natural processes. I seek no Spirit of God in Nature, because I believe that I perceive the nature of the human spirit in myself. I calmly admit my animal ancestry, because I be- <note>The sentence likely concludes with "believe" or "behold" on the following page, continuing the author's defense of evolutionary theory within a spiritual context.</note>

<summary>The author explains that true spiritual insight does not require projecting "souls" onto nature; instead, by finding the spirit within oneself, one can accept the purely physical descriptions of nature provided by scientists like Darwin and Haeckel.</summary>

<keywords>Nature, Spirit, Charles Darwin, Ernst Haeckel, sense-world, self-knowledge, animal ancestry, Philosophy of Freedom</keywords>

->277<-

->AFTERWORD<-

# AFTERWORD

lieve myself to know that there, where
these animal ancestors have their origin,
no spirit of like nature with soul can work.
I can only agree with Ernst Haeckel <note>Ernst Haeckel (1834–1919) was a prominent German biologist and philosopher who championed Darwinism, though his "Monist" philosophy was often seen as purely materialistic.</note> when
he prefers the “eternal rest of the grave”
to an immortality such as is taught by
some religions.<gloss>¹</gloss> For I find a dishonour-
ing of Spirit, an ugly sin against the Spirit,
in the conception of a soul continuing to
exist after the manner of a **sensible being** <term>sensible being: a being that exists in a form perceptible to the physical senses, like a body.</term>.

I hear a shrill discord when the scien-
tific facts in Haeckel’s presentation come
up against the “piety” of the **confessions** <note>In this context, "confessions" refers to the formal religious doctrines or denominations of the time.</note>
of some of our contemporaries. But
for me there rings out from confessions
of faith, which give a discord with natural
facts, naught of the spirit of the higher
piety which I find in Jacob Boehme <note>Jacob Boehme (1575–1624), a German Christian mystic whose work focused on the presence of God within the human soul and nature.</note>
and Angelus Silesius <note>Angelus Silesius (1624–1677), a German Catholic priest and physician known for his mystical poetry.</note>. This higher piety
stands far more in full harmony with

---

¹ <note>original: "Cp."</note> Compare Haeckel’s *The Riddle of the Universe*.

<summary>The author concludes that religious views of the soul as a physical-like entity after death are a "sin against the Spirit," arguing instead for a "higher piety" that remains in harmony with scientific facts like those of Ernst Haeckel.</summary>

<keywords>Ernst Haeckel, immortality, Jacob Boehme, Angelus Silesius, higher piety, The Riddle of the Universe, materialism, spiritual science</keywords>

--- Page 298 ---
<meta>This page completes the argument from page 277, asserting that modern natural science and mystical inner paths are not contradictory, concluding the work with a call to internalize these truths.</meta>

->278<-

# MYSTICS OF THE RENAISSANCE

the working of the natural. There lies no contradiction in the fact of saturating oneself with the knowledge of the most recent natural science, and at the same time treading the path which Jacob Boehme and Angelus Silesius have sought. He who enters on that path in the sense of those thinkers has no need to fear losing himself in a shallow **materialism** <term>materialism: the philosophical theory that nothing exists except matter and its movements and modifications.</term> when he lets the secrets of Nature be laid before him by a “natural history of creation.” <note>This refers to Ernst Haeckel’s *The Natural History of Creation* (1868), a major work that popularized Darwinian evolutionary theory in Germany.</note> Whoever has grasped my thoughts in this sense will understand with me in like manner the last saying of the *Cherubinean Wanderer* <note>A collection of mystical, rhymed couplets by Angelus Silesius, first published in 1657.</note>, with which also this book shall close: “Friend, it is even enough. In case thou more wilt read, go forth, and thyself become the book, thyself the reading.”

->THE END<-

<summary>The author concludes by reconciling modern evolutionary science with mysticism, suggesting that a true understanding of nature leads back to the soul. The book ends with a quote from Angelus Silesius urging the reader to live the truth rather than just read it.</summary>

<keywords>natural science, Jacob Boehme, Angelus Silesius, materialism, Natural History of Creation, Cherubinean Wanderer, mysticism, spiritual realization</keywords>
